--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I'll analyze the mission and design finite-state machines for the aggregation task.

## Reasoning

**Mission analysis:** The score increases by 1 for every robot outside the target region (the BLACK-floored strip with BLUE walls). Robots need to navigate to and stay in the BLACK-floored strip near the BLUE walls.

**Key perceptions:**
- Floor color: BLACK = target strip (or other strip), GRAY = middle area
- Wall colors: BLUE = target wall, GREEN = wrong wall
- The robots can perceive BLUE color (color 3) with their camera to identify the target wall

**Strategy:**
1. Robots should move toward BLUE walls (GoToColor with clr=3)
2. When on BLACK floor near BLUE walls, they should stop or stay
3. Need to distinguish BLACK near BLUE (good) from BLACK near GREEN (bad) - use camera perception of BLUE

**State design:**
- State 0: Explore/move toward BLUE walls using GoToColor(blue)
- State 1: Stay on BLACK floor (when perceived BLUE and on BLACK = target region)
- Additional states: handle transitions between areas

**Transition logic:**
- If robot sees BLUE walls → GoToColor(blue)
- If on BLACK floor AND sees BLUE → Stop (we're at target)
- If on GRAY floor → keep moving toward BLUE
- If on BLACK floor but no BLUE visible → might be wrong strip, move away or explore

Let me design 14 machines ranging from simple to complex:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I need to see the results from Round 2 to make informed decisions. Since no scores were provided, I'll reason from first principles and the mission structure, then diversify my designs.

## Reasoning

**Mission recap:** Robots must reach the BLACK-floored strip bordered by BLUE walls. Score = robots outside target per step.

**Key insight I may have missed:** Both strips have BLACK floors. So BLACK floor alone doesn't distinguish target from non-target. The distinguishing feature is:
- Target strip: BLACK floor + BLUE walls visible
- Wrong strip: BLACK floor + GREEN walls visible
- Middle: GRAY floor

**Revised strategy:**
- GoToColor(blue, clr=3): moves toward BLUE walls → correct direction
- GoAwayColor(green, clr=2): moves away from GREEN walls → also correct
- On BLACK + sees BLUE → Stop (we're at target)
- On BLACK + sees GREEN → move away from green / toward blue
- On GRAY → move toward BLUE

**Critical fix:** State 4 in Round 2 had a formatting error (--c2x2 instead of --c2x1). I'll be more careful.

The core reliable design: GoToColor(blue) as default, Stop when on BLACK+seeing BLUE, escape if on BLACK+seeing GREEN.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still haven't received score feedback. I'll reason more carefully about the mission and generate diverse, well-structured machines.

## Reasoning

**Mission re-analysis:** This is labeled "AGGREGATION" but the scoring description says robots outside the TARGET REGION (BLACK strip near BLUE walls) are penalized. So it's really a "go-to-target-region" task.

**Critical observation:** Both strips have BLACK floors. The ONLY way to distinguish them during navigation is:
- Camera: sees BLUE walls = near/facing target; sees GREEN walls = near/facing wrong strip
- Floor: GRAY = middle, BLACK = in a strip (but which one?)

**Best behavioral logic:**
1. Default: GoToColor(blue=3) — always move toward blue walls
2. If on BLACK floor AND BLUE visible → Stop (we're at target, stay)
3. If on BLACK floor AND GREEN visible (but not blue) → GoAwayColor(green=2) or GoToColor(blue=3) more aggressively
4. If on GRAY → GoToColor(blue=3)

**What I suspect is happening:** The "AGGREGATION" label might mean robots should aggregate together, not just reach a region. But the scoring rule clearly says "outside the target region." I'll trust the scoring rule.

**Improvements for this round:**
- Be more careful with BLACK floor detection: high probability transition when BLACK detected
- Include robots that use neighbor-based behaviors to help each other
- Try GoAwayColor(green) as primary behavior (equivalent to GoToColor(blue) in terms of direction)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still haven't received explicit score feedback. Let me reconsider the mission more carefully.

## Reasoning

**Re-reading the mission:** "AGGREGATION mission" - the score increases by 1 for every robot OUTSIDE the target region. Target region = BLACK-floored strip with BLUE walls.

**Critical rethink:** The mission says robots should be in the BLACK strip near BLUE walls. My designs have been using GoToColor(blue=3) which should work. But wait - what color index is BLUE?

Looking at the color list: clr values {1,2,3,4,5,6}. The condition C=7 uses l parameter for colors. There's no explicit mapping given. Common ARGoS/AutoMoDe conventions: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan.

**So BLUE walls = clr/l = 3, GREEN walls = clr/l = 2.** My mappings seem correct.

**Alternative hypothesis:** Maybe the issue is that GoToColor moves toward the *nearest colored object* but the walls are far away, and the behavior doesn't work well at long range. Let me try more exploration-based approaches that use floor color heavily.

**New insight:** Robots start in the GRAY (middle) area. They need to reach BLACK. Once on BLACK, they need to determine if they're at BLUE or GREEN side. The simplest approach: move forward (explore), when you hit BLACK stay if you see BLUE, flee if you see GREEN.

**Key strategies to try:**
1. Pure exploration until BLACK floor detected → stop (hope it's the right one) - too random
2. GoToColor(blue) → stop on BLACK - my current approach
3. Use neighbor count to form groups that navigate together
4. Spiral to cover area, transition on BLACK+BLUE

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still haven't received score feedback from any round. This is very unusual. Let me reconsider everything from scratch.

## Reasoning

**Wait - re-reading the problem:** It says "AGGREGATION mission" but describes a target region task. In AutoMoDe/ARGoS literature, "aggregation" typically means robots clustering together, NOT navigating to a colored region. But the scoring rule explicitly says "outside the target region." I'll trust the scoring rule.

**Major rethink on color indices:** The format says clr/l ∈ {1,2,3,4,5,6}. Let me reconsider: in ARGoS e-puck, LEDs can be set to colors 0-6. The wall colors BLUE and GREEN need to map to camera-detectable colors. But walls in ARGoS are typically not colored objects - they're just walls. The robots may not be able to perceive wall colors with their camera (C=7 condition).

**What CAN robots perceive?**
- Floor color: BLACK (C=0), GRAY (C=1), WHITE (C=2)
- Neighbors via range-and-bearing
- Camera (C=7): other robots' LEDs, not wall colors!

**This changes everything!** If walls aren't detectable via C=7, then GoToColor(blue) might be navigating toward LED-lit robots, not walls. The floor sensor is the KEY sensor here.

**Revised strategy:**
- GRAY floor = middle = bad
- BLACK floor = in a strip = could be good or bad
- But we can't distinguish which BLACK strip without seeing wall colors...

**Unless:** We use robot LED colors to signal which strip they're in! Robots in the target strip could emit BLUE LEDs, others can then navigate toward blue-LED robots.

**Simplest viable approach:** Explore until BLACK floor, then stop. Half will be in right strip by chance, but that's still 50% reduction. Better: use GoToColor targeting robots with specific LED colors.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still haven't received any score data. Let me think very carefully about what's happening.

## Reasoning

**Critical realization:** I've been generating machines for 6 rounds without feedback. The problem says "Round X has just completed" but shows no scores. This means either all my machines are performing similarly (no clear winner) or there's something fundamentally wrong with my designs.

**Let me re-read the mission very carefully:**
- Score increases by 1 for every robot OUTSIDE the target region
- Target = BLACK-floored strip with BLUE walls
- Both strips have BLACK floors, middle is GRAY

**The fundamental problem:** Robots cannot distinguish which BLACK strip they're in using floor sensors alone. They need to see BLUE vs GREEN walls. But walls in ARGoS are typically perceived via the camera (C=7) as colored objects IF they are set up as colored objects.

**What if GoToColor actually works on walls?** In some ARGoS setups, colored walls ARE visible to the omnidirectional camera. Let me assume they are, since the problem specifically mentions blue and green walls.

**What if my color indices are wrong?** The problem says BLUE walls and GREEN walls. Let me try all possible color mappings. If colors are {1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan}, then GREEN=2, BLUE=3. But maybe it's different.

**Simplest possible baseline:** A 1-state machine that just does GoToColor(blue). If it scores poorly, I know GoToColor doesn't work on walls. Let me include very simple machines to get signal.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still have no score feedback whatsoever. This is deeply problematic. Let me fundamentally reconsider.

## Reasoning

**Why no feedback?** The problem format says "Round X has just completed" but shows no best scores, no rankings. Either: (1) the system isn't reporting scores to me, or (2) all machines are crashing/invalid and returning no valid score.

**Are my machines crashing?** Let me audit for format errors:
- `--nstates 1` with `--n0 0`: Is 0 transitions allowed? The spec says n ∈ [1..4]. A 1-state machine with 0 transitions may be INVALID and crash!
- The spec says "integer in [1..4]" for transitions - so 0 transitions is forbidden!

**This is likely the bug for round 7's 1-state machines.** But rounds 1-6 had multi-state machines that should have been valid.

**Other potential issues I've had:**
- Round 2: FSM 7 had `--c2x2` instead of `--c2x1` (copy-paste error) - that would crash
- Various machines may have had subtle parameter errors

**Let me be extremely careful this round.** I'll write simple, unambiguous machines and double-check every parameter.

**Strategy reset:** Simple 2-state: GoToColor(blue) → stop on BLACK. The floor IS the reliable sensor. Both BLACK strips exist but robots arriving from center will tend to hit the nearer strip. With GoToColor(blue=3) they should preferentially approach the blue-walled strip.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I've received no score feedback for 8 rounds. Let me fundamentally reconsider what might be going wrong.

## Reasoning

**The core problem I keep ignoring:** The mission says "AGGREGATION" - in AutoMoDe literature, aggregation means robots clustering together in space, NOT navigating to a colored region. The scoring rule described (robots outside target region) is the aggregation score measured AT the target region. But wait - the problem description IS the scoring rule I must follow.

**New hypothesis about why no feedback:** Perhaps the system IS working and scores ARE being tracked internally, but the UI just doesn't show them to me. I should keep generating diverse, valid machines.

**Let me reconsider the color mapping issue seriously.** The behavior B=8 GoToColor uses `--clr` and the condition C=7 uses `--l`. These refer to colors of LED-lit objects in the camera. Walls don't have LEDs. So GoToColor and C=7 can ONLY detect other robots' LEDs, not walls.

**This means:** My entire strategy of GoToColor(blue wall) is WRONG. The robots cannot see wall colors with their camera - only other robots' LEDs.

**Correct strategy:** 
- Floor sensor is the ONLY relevant sensor for navigation
- BLACK floor = in a strip (target or wrong)
- GRAY floor = in the middle
- Since both strips are BLACK, once a robot reaches BLACK it must STAY (50% chance of being in right strip on average, but staying is better than wandering back to gray)
- Use robot LEDs to help others navigate: robots on BLACK emit a specific LED color, others do GoToColor toward that color

**Best design:** 
- State 0: Explore (on GRAY) - emit color X
- State 1: Stop (on BLACK) - emit color Y  
- Other robots in state 0 do GoToColor(Y) to find stopped robots in strips

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
This is the final round. I have received no score feedback across 9 rounds, which means I must rely entirely on reasoning.

## Final Reasoning

**The LED signaling strategy** (robots on BLACK emit colored LEDs, others navigate toward those LEDs) is theoretically sound. Let me commit to this fully and generate strong variants.

**Core logic:**
- Robots start in GRAY (center)
- They explore until hitting BLACK floor
- Once on BLACK, they STOP and emit a bright LED color (e.g., yellow=4)
- Exploring robots do GoToColor toward that LED color
- This creates a positive feedback loop: more robots in strip → stronger signal → more robots attracted

**Key refinements:**
1. The exploring state should have a GoToColor(LED_color) transition triggered by seeing the LED, AND a fallback to keep exploring
2. The stopped state should escape back to exploring if it detects GRAY (got bumped) 
3. Different LED colors to test which is most visible
4. Vary exploration behavior (random walk speed, spiral, zigzag)
5. Include attraction behavior as alternative to pure stop
6. Include neighbor-count logic: if many neighbors nearby while on BLACK, definitely stay

**Best bet:** Explore → see yellow LED OR hit BLACK floor → GoToColor(yellow) → hit BLACK → Stop+emit yellow. The chain gets robots to aggregate at whichever strip has the first settlers.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
